This can provide a great deal of flexibility and power, but also introduces a number of challenges and complexities.\n\nOne reason why fexprs are not considered trivial is that they can lead to a number of subtle and difficult-to-debug issues. For example, because fexprs can manipulate their arguments as unevaluated expressions, they can introduce order-of-evaluation issues and other forms of non-determinism. {"sample_id": "helpsteer3:train:0cc76a988b1d98598a30a7d776ffe75122c827fd9727747330c1a6c8b7d7d134", "task": "What's mock ruritarian comedy?", "chosen": "Mock Ruritanian comedy is a sub-genre of comedy that satirizes or parodies the conventions of Ruritanian romance, a genre of literature and film that involves stories set in fictional countries, often in Central or Eastern Europe, with exotic-sounding names like \"Ruritania\". These stories often involve royalty, intrigue, romance, and adventure.\n\nIn a mock Ruritanian comedy, these elements are exaggerated or played for laughs. The genre often pokes fun at the idealized, romanticized portrayal of these fictional countries and their societies. It can also satirize the conventions of the genre itself, such as the use of stock characters, predictable plotlines, and melodramatic situations.\n\nExamples of mock Ruritanian comedies include the 1984 film \"Top Secret!\", which parodies both Ruritanian romance and Elvis Presley movies, and the 1993 film \"Dave\", which satirizes the political aspects of the genre.", "rejected": "Mock Ruritarian comedy is a subgenre of comedy that parodies the style and tropes of the British sitcom \"The Vicar of Dibley\". The show, created by Paul Mayhew-Archer and Richard Curtis, is known for its witty dialogue, eccentric characters, and satirical take on small-town life and the Church of England.\n\nThe term \"Mock Ruritarian\" is a play on words, combining \"mock\" (meaning to imitate in a humorous way) and \"Ruritarian\" (a term used to describe the fictional rural setting of \"The Vicar of Dibley\").
